| Object and linking (§§9.1–9.3) | One ISA object's closed sections and linked symbols; no native-container schema. | Publish a versioned inventory associating embedded/linked ISA object bytes and object_hash with selected device roots and certificates. A native-container hash, if used, is a separate identity. |
| Certificates/inventory (§§12.1–12.3) | Closure-keyed device-region records; current text does not explicitly delimit native inventory. | Insert the shared scoping rule in §12.3, cross-reference from §9.2, and require complete device-owner/link-proof coverage per root. Multiple device roots can share static closures without merging root-relative contexts. |
| Launch/entry (§10) | bennett_run(program, cc_entry, args, entropy) starts device CC; it is not native main. | Publish a host call/return shim and container/root selection/feature negotiation. Define native argument/result marshalling, including ptr (whose host binding §10 does not specify), status/fault propagation, suspend/reverse/close and ownership. Do not invent a current general launch shim. |
| Budgets (§5.4; §§10–11) | Validated token tuples and identity scoped to a program/run; action_call has no primary token (§4.7). | Bind selected device invocation budgets and token equality to the launch request; charge actual device/replay work. State host/shim accounting separately; a native process has no invented global B. |
| Handles (§3.3; §10; dialect §6.6) | Register ids refer to the loaded program's ledger; run ownership and token validity are explicit. | Map host-visible opaque handles to that program/ledger with validity and cross-launch ownership checks; preserve class vectors and token identities. Do not serialize native pointers as device ids or token tuples as identities. |
| Effect tape (§7.3; §10) | Run-associated R effects and explicit copied hostcall requests; no implicit log of native host I/O. | Associate each device run and tape with the launched root and define host effect/proxy ordering and replay at the boundary, preserving §7.4 reversal. Saved-journal interchange needs its own versioned replay profile. |
| Host memory by reference (§§7.1–7.2; dialect §§8.5, 17.1, 19.4–19.5) | Device layout plus dialect snapshot/replay and boundary-address/promotion rules; copied hostcall bytes do not provide general shared pointers. | Publish the mapping and lifetime protocol named in the statement, including allocator metadata/history and pointer-rich layouts, so ordinary host memory can be supplied safely without universal host promotion. Retain existing snapshot/replay as a construction, not the sole eventual capability. |

For example native main can call marked roots A and B in distinct ISA objects.
Each has its own object/root certificate and root context []; neither
certificate inventories main or host-only libc. A shared linked device
callee is checked in each root's actual invocation tree. A native comparison
executes the corresponding materialised region/callee closure under its
recorded layout, input and tool configuration (dialect §§17.7, 19.5, 19.9).

These notes specify boundary scope and exact remaining contract work; they
implement no container, host shim or memory registration. The existing
bennett-4ht issue tracks that publication/implementation obligation. Current
device contracts and their complete estimate/proof requirements remain in
force; an unimplemented boundary is not evidence that mixed execution works.


# The document under review (round 1 snapshot)

# b-isa-spec-sqr — mcx controls named by a bundle

Council proposal, not an ISA implementation. References:
[ISA 0.9](../../../spec/Bennett-ISA-Spec-0.9.md) §§3.3 (indexed operands,
distinctness), 4.4 (gate forms), 11.1 (resolved gate wires), 16.2 (grammar).

## Exact defect and North Star scope

§4.4 writes `mcx (c_1..c_k) t` and “X on the target iff all controls
are |1>”. It does not say whether a NAME in that list must denote one
wire or may denote a wire[N] bundle. §3.3 defines bundles and indexed
operands, but supplies no gate-specific list expansion rule. §16.2's
parenthesized list admits bare NAME entries; it does not admit indexed
entries such as `(c[0], c[1])`. Thus the usual informal advice to list
those indices is not an existing grammar solution.

This is an **operand contract ambiguity**, not evidence that the abstract
machine lacks a many-controlled X gate. Explicit `ccx c[0] c[1] p` already
expresses the two-control case. North Star §§3.1, 4.2, 5.1 requires the
operation and interoperable interpretation, not this particular shorthand.
Either precisely defined expansion or a precisely stated scalar-only
syntax can preserve the machine capability. Expansion is recommended
because it preserves the admitted bundle spelling and needs no new list
grammar. Reading only the first bit has no defensible all-controls meaning.

## Alternatives and concrete witness

Read-only witness:
`../bennett-conformance/checker-replay/witnesses/mcx-bundle-control/`.
Its action has p,q:wire mut and c:wire[2] in, then:

```text
swap p q
mcx (c) p
```

With p=q=0, c=1 (bits c[0]=1,c[1]=0), expansion leaves p=0;
first-bit execution produces p=1. With c=3 expansion produces p=1.
The complete forward map is (p,q,c) -> (q XOR [c=3],p,c).
The adjoint applies the controlled X before the swap, restoring every
input. It is generally not the same source sequence run forward twice.

| Alternative | Meaning and cost |
|---|---|
| Flatten each bundle entry in increasing bit index | All named controls participate; k is the total wire count. Recommended. |
| Require every entry to be scalar | Legal alternative if explicitly published; bundles require another spelling/construction, and indexed list syntax would need a grammar amendment. |
| Broadcast independent X operations per control | XOR of controls, not conjunction; wrong at c=1 and c=3. |
| Inspect c[0] alone | Silently loses controls; wrong at c=1. |

## Recommended exact spec diff

Insert immediately after the forms sentence in §4.4:

```text
Each NAME entry of an mcx control list may denote wire or wire[N].
It contributes every wire of that bundle, in increasing bit index;
concatenate entries in written list order. The k in mcx(k) is the
number of resulting control wires, not the number of list entries.
The target denotes exactly one wire (a scalar name or indexed operand).
Apply §3.3's pairwise distinctness to the resulting control wires and
target before execution; overlapping or repeated entries reject as
ill-formed, never deduplicate them to repair an operand list. Existing
effective predicate-context rules still apply. The empty list has
zero explicit controls and applies X under the active predicate.
Flattening determines resolved primitive operands, gate cost and
realisation arity, but does not rewrite source identity or source points.
```

Append the normative witness:

```text
For c:wire[2], mcx (c) p has the same gate semantics as
ccx c[0] c[1] p: it changes p exactly for c=3, not c=1 or c=2.
mcx (c, c) p and a target c[0] with controls (c) are ill-formed.
```

No general §16.2 list-grammar change is required. This proposal does not
add indexed entries inside parenthesized lists; use the existing indexed
operands of ccx for the scalar two-bit comparison above. It also does
not legalise a multiwire target or change cx/ccx into bundle operations.

## Hashes, catalog and consumers

Retain the written bundle operand in source envelopes. Under an explicitly
authorized same-version repair, existing object/interface/closure hashes
are unchanged; the published witness binds object
`db3d3f92a79077074901449d83532f1a0d82ef1621bd833adb7ad14d1aedd467`
and action closure
`e38d5da64f9a0ae89a66243bcbe5a321cb807a5bbabf83952b63d3465dfab346`.
They identify bytes, not an implementation's formerly mistaken unitary.
Schedules that counted one control must be regenerated and certificates
rechecked; unchanged source hashes do not validate old execution caches.

The ref catalog's existing mcx wire-list recipes already operate on
resolved wires. Their bytes need no change: source adapters must supply
all flattened controls and k. If an adapter changes generated leaves,
update its expected leaf hashes and affected schedules, not `ref@0.9`
silently. K78 primitive identity uses the complete effective control set;
no new causal-matching exception is required. A 0.10 publication changes
version-prefixed envelopes and pins as described in [INDEX](INDEX.md).

## Conformance vectors and actual verification

The recorded older published-checker result rejects the witness table
because it flips p at c=1; the stored replay result proves all 16 inputs.
Re-ran the current replay CLI with `--max-elements 16`: exit 0,
claim proof, 16 elements, covered_fraction 1. Its separate unchecked
metadata obligations remain unchecked; this is only the relation claim.

Built the current reference interpreter read-only into the job target.
Its `check` on the **original** bundle object exits 1 with
`§3.3: c must name one wire`. It does not reproduce the older checker's
first-bit result. Current validation and action execution require scalar
list entries. That is evidence for the ambiguity, not proof that the
recommended expansion is implemented.

For an executable independent comparison, changed only a scratch copy
to `ccx c[0] c[1] p` and recomputed its declared interface hash. Reference
check/hash pass; object hash is
`c785ff3f310f968e2a26f68834e99ab892cf0d1bffe816cf26b17d45c33a65af`,
interface/closure hash
`f53238499e07824b6c62385c33426d4bc56bfaf900df61264ec65e0589061f39`.
All 16 forward and 16 adjoint basis cases pass the map above, with c
preserved. An initial attempt at `(c[0], c[1])` was rejected by the
current list grammar; it is not used as a passing witness. Reproduction
is in [INDEX](INDEX.md). No source interpreter or sibling fixture changed.

Acceptance adds bundle widths 1/2/3, mixed scalar/bundle entries, list
order, c=0/1/2/3, empty-list X, target/duplicate/control alias negatives,
active predicate and adjoint cases. Check exact +1 phase and a superposed
control entangled with a reference, full flattened arity/cost, source
hash stability and K78 matching against the equivalent scalar primitive.
Suite F must distinguish an operand rejection from a replay mismatch;
suite C/T6 checks the resulting unitary.

## K entry and publication route

Proposed row: `K(new) | §§3.3,4.4,11.1 | MCX-BUNDLE-CONTROLS: ordered
bundle expansion; distinctness and k over denoted wires; scalar target |
sqr | operand/expansion semantics`. Reserve no number. Recommend a
council-authorized 0.9 K repair only if the ambiguity-repair track is
explicitly approved; otherwise publish in **0.10** under the semantic
version rule. Do not silently extend accepted 0.9 syntax or claim all
current consumers already agree. The scalar-only alternative is a valid
decision if the council instead supplies its exact operand wording and
preserves the general many-control capability. Keep sqr open meanwhile.

## Mixed fat object

[fat-object-scope.md](../2026-11-02-rev2/fat-object-scope.md) and North Star
§§2, 3.3, 4.4 apply. Flatten within each marked device module's actual
wire bindings; wires and certificate domains do not include native host
code. Gate arity and budgets belong to that device invocation. Shared
device helpers may be linked under several roots without making native
main or host-only libc certificate regions. No launch, opaque-handle or
tape format changes follow from this operand rule. The shared note's
container/root and mixed-launch boundary additions remain required.

name: beads
description: Use when working in a repository that uses bd or Beads for durable project task tracking, issue dependencies, blocker management, multi-session handoff, or shared work memory. # Beads

Use Beads as the shared project task system. Local plans, scratch files, and personal memories are useful, but they are not the durable source of truth for project work.

## First Step

Run:

```bash
bd prime
```

If that prints nothing, check whether the repository has an active Beads workspace:

```bash
bd where
```

## Preferred Route

Use the `bd` CLI when shell access is available. It is the most compact and direct Beads interface.

## Core CLI Workflow

1. Find work:

```bash
bd ready
bd list --status=open
bd list --status=in_progress
```

2. Inspect before editing:

```bash
bd show <id>
```

3. Claim work atomically:

```bash
bd update <id> --claim
```

4. Create durable follow-up work when implementation reveals new tasks:

```bash
bd create "Short title" --description="Why this exists and what needs to be done" --type=task --priority=2
```

5. Close completed work:

```bash
bd close <id> --reason="Completed"
```

## What Belongs In Beads

Use Beads for:

- shared project tasks
- blockers and dependencies
- discovered follow-up work
- work that must survive thread reset, compaction, or handoff
- status that another person or agent should be able to resume

Use agent-local planning tools only for the current turn's execution checklist. Do not treat them as shared project state.

## Rules

- Do not create markdown TODO files as the source of truth when Beads is available.
- Do not use `bd edit`; it opens an interactive editor. Use `bd update` flags instead.
- Prefer `--json` when parsing `bd` output programmatically.
- If hooks are installed, `bd prime` may already be injected. Run it manually when context is missing.
- Do not auto-close or mutate tasks unless the work is actually complete.

# Contract derivations 3 — council decision index

Five proposals, not published spec changes or consumer acceptance. Read
COMMON, repository CLAUDE/AGENTS, the Beads skill/prime, two first-pass
notes and two pass2 notes before derivation. All five full issue records
were read with bd. Direct sibling reads, including --readonly, failed
at the read-only embedded Dolt LOCK. Copies of the actual embedded
databases under `.tmp/sol-derive3/` then permitted `bd --readonly show`
for 4yg.1 and brt-vbi. No passive JSONL was imported or substituted for
the database. Source issues remain open for council/implementation work.

| Issue | Determination | Recommended publication |
|---|---|---|
| [bennett-arith-4yg.1](bennett-arith-4yg.1.md) | Valid FMA and binary128-conversion midpoints have no even candidate under the printed PPC rule; flags separately violate an existing rule | Dialect §13.1 parity repair in next 0.2 patch and 0.3 draft; no ISA K |
| [brt-vbi](brt-vbi.md) | Published all-width helper requirements lack a public delivery/collection protocol; actual i257 bodies also missing | Dialect §§12.8/19.1 offline interface and fixed point; no new ISA opcode/K |
| [b-isa-spec-pe1](b-isa-spec-pe1.md) | K13 example invents a borrowed lifetime that K70 rejects | 0.9 K13 example erratum: empty borrowed arrays, 24-bit D |
| [b-isa-spec-2qc](b-isa-spec-2qc.md) | Executor preflight and proof owner exist; certificate checker coverage duty is ambiguous | Explicit owner/site/context rule, coordinated with 0jq; council-authorized 0.9 K repair or 0.10 |
| [b-isa-spec-sqr](b-isa-spec-sqr.md) | Bundle entries in an mcx list have no explicit operand rule; current implementations disagree | Ordered bundle expansion, full wire arity/distinctness; council-authorized 0.9 K repair or 0.10 |

None is an excuse to reduce a format/width domain, omit an inverse
obligation, or declare a North Star capability inert. K13 is an example
defect rather than a new machine capability gap. The remaining findings
concern deterministic contract interpretation or delivery. All existing
SQLite, Julia, libc, reversible execution and demo-ladder obligations
remain. Existing beads and cross-repo requests already track the decisions;
no duplicate task or sibling tracker mutation is needed for this pass.

## Publication and combined decisions

[spec/README.md](../../../spec/README.md) requires a full version change
for semantic changes and council review. “0.9 K repair” in these notes
means a proposed use of the existing council repair process, **not
authority to silently amend a published contract**. Without explicit
in-version authorization, the 2qc/sqr semantic additions go to 0.10.
New version prefixes change affected object/interface/closure envelopes,
example identities and consumer pins. A same-version repair with unchanged
source bytes preserves their source identities but still invalidates
incompatible execution/schedule/proof assumptions. `ref@0.9` changes only
if its own catalog bytes/version change; none of these proposals requires
such a rewrite. Assign numeric K entries only at acceptance.

PPC parity and runtime delivery belong to the dialect because they define
LLVM numerical/helper lowering and build inputs. ISA 0.9/0.10 need no
new K for either. Publish the exact proposed dialect text in the next
available maintained patch and mirror it in 0.3; the proposal is not a
private runtime/driver agreement. The new helper protocol is itself
versioned with schema 1, independent of ISA subformat numbers.

For 2qc use the existing concrete invocation-context vocabulary, owner
closure selection and owner-local claim indices. Coordinate with
[pass2 0jq](../2026-11-02-pass2/b-isa-spec-0jq.md); do not create a second
inverse proof owner or replace tuple applicability by root budget equality.
For sqr retain the written source identity and resolve complete wire
operands before K78 primitive matching and scheduling. A parenthesized
indexed list is not currently a legal substitute under §16.2.

## Mixed fat object

Every note cites and applies
[fat-object-scope.md](../2026-11-02-rev2/fat-object-scope.md), binding under
bennett-4ht and advisor 08:45Z (North Star §§2, 3.3, 4.4). A native host
binary remains native; only marked device modules and their required
linked closures become ISA objects. Helper generation, comparison,
certificate inventory and invocation coverage bind those device roots,
with a separate root-relative context [] per root. They do not inventory
native main, host-only argv/envp setup or host-only libc. The shared note
names the exact remaining container/root/certificate association, launch,
budget/handle, tape/run and host-memory mapping changes. This pass neither
implements those mechanisms nor locks them out.

The runtime modules actually audited had SHA-256
`cc8f318f2caf9d989ea64932cd177c3bf32e6c65c156b9949d3b49e4b94c999f`
(LLVM18) and
`964ea16513db1d54adbaa5a4c8124b1ac731fee8f487f34dc6e9e12bb9df5642`
(LLVM23). These are observed evidence pins, not a request to re-pin
consumers to missing helper bodies. The proposed canonical request
example in the finite block hashes to
`74e7a75be203bc350a18a84980258fa09a3e9936837e8aa67fb01532064cdd81`;
it is a wire-format example, not an implemented generator result.

The PPC script loads the producer's existing native test libraries. This
pass does not freshly compile them from the publication or infer full
arith conformance from them; its exact rational defect proof is independent
of their answers. Stored older checker verdicts are identified as stored
evidence, distinct from the newly built reference result. The focused
replay tests do not implement the proposed nested-context checker rule.

## Reproducible finite checks

Run the following shell block after the environment prefix. It extracts
the Python below from this index and verifies the actual notes plus
concrete witnesses; it creates only a scratch ccx object. The outer memory
bound is inherited by all child commands.

```python
from pathlib import Path
from fractions import Fraction as F
from itertools import product
import hashlib, json, os, re, subprocess, sys

ROOT = Path('/home/tobias/Projects/bennett-workspace/bennett-isa-spec')
WS = ROOT.parent
OUT = ROOT / 'docs/notes/2026-11-02-derive3'
JOB = ROOT / '.tmp/sol-derive3'
REF = JOB / 'target/debug/bennett-isa-ref'
REPLAY = WS / 'bennett-conformance/checker-replay'
WITNESS = REPLAY / 'witnesses/mcx-bundle-control'
assert os.environ['TMPDIR'] == str(JOB / 'tmp')
assert os.environ['CARGO_TARGET_DIR'] == str(JOB / 'target')
assert not any(k in os.environ for k in
               ('DISPLAY', 'WAYLAND_DISPLAY', 'DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS', 'BROWSER'))
ids = ['bennett-arith-4yg.1', 'brt-vbi', 'b-isa-spec-pe1',
       'b-isa-spec-2qc', 'b-isa-spec-sqr']
assert {p.name for p in OUT.iterdir()} == {i+'.md' for i in ids} | {'INDEX.md'}
index = (OUT / 'INDEX.md').read_text()
for ident in ids:
    s = (OUT / (ident+'.md')).read_text()
    assert ident in s and not re.search(r'\b(?:TODO|TBD)\b', s)
    for heading in ('defect', 'north star', 'alternatives', 'recommended exact',
                    'hashes', 'conformance', 'k entry', 'mixed fat object'):
        assert heading in s.lower(), (ident, heading)
    assert 'fat-object-scope.md' in s
    assert index.count('| ['+ident+']') == 1
for p in OUT.glob('*.md'):
    s = p.read_text()
    assert sum(line.startswith('```') for line in s.splitlines()) % 2 == 0, p
    prose = re.sub(r'```[^\n]*\n.*?\n```', '', s, flags=re.S)
    for link in re.findall(r'\[[^\]]+\]\(([^)]+)\)', prose):
        if not link.startswith(('http://', 'https://', '#')):
            assert (p.parent / link.split('#')[0]).exists(), (p, link)
print('PASS: five proposals and index; sections, links, fences, fat-object citations')

def pow2(n):
    return F(2**n) if n >= 0 else F(1, 2**(-n))
def decode(bits, fraction_bits, exponent_bits, bias):
    sign = -1 if bits >> (fraction_bits+exponent_bits) else 1
    exp = (bits >> fraction_bits) & ((1 << exponent_bits)-1)
    frac = bits & ((1 << fraction_bits)-1)
    assert exp != (1 << exponent_bits)-1
    sig = frac if exp == 0 else (1 << fraction_bits) | frac
    return sign * sig * pow2((1-bias if exp == 0 else exp-bias)-fraction_bits)
def f64(bits):
    return decode(bits, 52, 11, 1023)
u = pow2(-1074)
h = f64(0x3ff0000000000001)
assert h == 1+pow2(-52)
exact = (h+u)*F(1,2) + f64(0x3fe0000000000001)
assert exact == h+u/2
H = f64(0x0030000000000001)
V = decode(0x3c030000000000001200000000000000, 112, 15, 16383)
assert V == H+u/2 and H > pow2(-1022)
for hi, v in ((h, exact), (H, V)):
    assert (hi/u).denominator == 1
    assert v-hi == hi+u-v == u/2
    assert hi+u-hi == u
# The two high raw significands and the nonzero low significand are odd.
assert (0x3ff0000000000001 & 1) == (0x0030000000000001 & 1) == 1
assert (0 & 1, 1 & 1) == (0, 1)  # proposed common low comparison selects zero
# Both-low-zero subnormal neighbours still use high parity.
assert f64(2)-f64(1) == u and (1 & 1, 2 & 1) == (1, 0)
print('PASS: independent exact PPC FMA/conversion midpoints and parity distinction')

spec = (ROOT / 'spec/Bennett-ISA-Spec-0.9.md').read_text()
block = spec.split('**CLAIM-REPLAY witness**', 1)[1].split('```json\n', 1)[1].split('\n```', 1)[0]
assert block.count('"borrowed":["1"]') == 6
fixed = block.replace('"borrowed":["1"]', '"borrowed":[]')
fixed = fixed.replace('"borrowed":[1]', '"borrowed":[]')
fixed = fixed.replace('1/33554432', '1/16777216')
k13 = json.loads(fixed)
tuples = [k13['row']['before'], k13['row']['after'],
          k13['wrong_row']['before'], k13['wrong_row']['after'],
          k13['changed_arg_after'], k13['short_budget_after']]
assert all(t['borrowed'] == [] and t['regions'] == [['2','1']] for t in tuples)
assert k13['widths']['borrowed'] == []
bits = sum(k13['widths']['bundles']) + sum(sum(r) for r in k13['widths']['regions'])
assert bits == 24 and str(F(1, 1 << bits)) == k13['covered_fraction']
assert 2 ^ ((1+2) % 4) == 1 and 2 ^ ((1+1) % 4) == 0
assert (2 ^ 0, 3 ^ 1) == (2, 2)
assert k13['sample_count'] == 4 and k13['distinct_samples'] == 1
print('PASS: all six K13 tuple repairs; 24-bit domain and exact singleton coverage')

# A finite coverage counterexample, not an implementation of the checker.
minimum = (3,0,0,0)
def covers(tk, arg, domain=range(4), token_arg=None):
    return (all(a <= b for a,b in zip(minimum,tk)) and arg in domain
            and (token_arg is None or token_arg == (3,0,0,0)))
assert covers((3,0,0,0),1) and covers((9,0,0,0),1)
assert not covers((2,0,0,0),1)
assert not covers((3,0,0,0),3,range(3))
assert not covers((9,0,0,0),1,token_arg=(4,0,0,0))
contexts = [(3,0,0,0), (2,0,0,0)]
assert [covers(t,1) for t in contexts] == [True,False]
assert {('G','H'), ('H','G')} == {('H','G'), ('G','H')}
print('PASS: qinplace minimum, exact-token, args-domain and two-context counterexamples')

# Proposed wire-request example; arithmetic is independent of any runtime body.
mask = (1 << 257)-1
a, b = (1 << 256)+3, 2
assert ((a*b) & mask) == 6
assert a.bit_count() == 3 and (((a*b) & mask).bit_count()) == 2
assert [min(64,257-64*k) for k in range(5)] == [64,64,64,64,1]
entries = [{'op':'mul','width':257}, {'op':'ctpop','width':257}, {'op':'mul','width':257}]
canonical_entries = [{'op':op,'width':w} for op,w in sorted({(e['op'],e['width']) for e in entries})]
assert canonical_entries == [{'op':'ctpop','width':257},{'op':'mul','width':257}]
request = {'schema':'bennett-runtimes-instantiations-1', 'llvm_major':18,
           'target_triple':'x86_64-unknown-linux-musl', 'data_layout':'e-p:64:64',
           'entries':canonical_entries}
canonical = json.dumps(request,sort_keys=True,separators=(',',':')).encode()
print('Proposed request example SHA-256:',hashlib.sha256(canonical).hexdigest())
print('PASS: i257 top-limb arithmetic and canonical request example')

def run(argv, cwd=ROOT, timeout=20, expected=0):
    p = subprocess.run([str(x) for x in argv], cwd=cwd, text=True,
                       capture_output=True, timeout=timeout)
    assert p.returncode == expected, (argv,p.returncode,p.stdout,p.stderr)
    return p
native = run([sys.executable,'-I','-B','-u',WS/'bennett-arith/tests/ppc_zero_low_witness.py'])
assert native.stdout.count('flags=0;') == 2
assert native.stdout.count('flags=24;') == 2
print(native.stdout, end='')
tests = run([sys.executable,'-B','-m','unittest','-v',
             'tests.test_replay.K13Witness','tests.test_replay.QinplaceCoverage'],cwd=REPLAY)
assert 'Ran 12 tests' in tests.stderr and tests.stderr.rstrip().endswith('OK')
print('PASS: independent replay focused tests, 12/12')
runtime = WS/'bennett-runtimes'
ll = runtime/'.tmp/sol-runtimes-queue/tmp/runtime-width-request.ll'
for major in (18,23):
    tools = Path(f'/usr/lib/llvm-{major}/bin')
    run([tools/'opt','-passes=verify','-disable-output',ll])
    bc = runtime/f'dist/llvm{major}/bennett-runtimes.bc'
    nm = run([tools/'llvm-nm','--defined-only',bc]).stdout
    defs = {line.split()[-1] for line in nm.splitlines() if line.split()}
    assert not {'__bennett_rt_i257_mul','__bennett_rt_i257_ctpop'} & defs
    print('PASS: LLVM',major,'i257 source verifies; both definitions absent;',
          'runtime SHA-256',hashlib.sha256(bc.read_bytes()).hexdigest())

replay = json.loads(run([sys.executable,'-B','-m','checker_replay',
                        WITNESS/'certificate.json','--object',WITNESS/'object.bisa',
                        '--max-elements','16'],cwd=REPLAY).stdout)
claim = next(iter(replay['regions'].values()))['claims'][0]
assert (claim['outcome'],claim['elements'],claim['covered_fraction']) == ('proof',16,'1')
original = run([REF,'check',WITNESS/'object.bisa'],expected=1)
assert 'c must name one wire' in original.stderr
scratch = JOB/'tmp/mcx-ccx.bisa'
source = (WITNESS/'object.bisa').read_text()
old = 'e38d5da64f9a0ae89a66243bcbe5a321cb807a5bbabf83952b63d3465dfab346'
new = 'f53238499e07824b6c62385c33426d4bc56bfaf900df61264ec65e0589061f39'
assert source.count('mcx (c) p') == 1 and source.count(old) == 1
scratch.write_text(source.replace('mcx (c) p','ccx c[0] c[1] p').replace(old,new))
for command in ('check','hash'):
    result = json.loads(run([REF,command,scratch]).stdout)
    assert result['object_hash'] == 'c785ff3f310f968e2a26f68834e99ab892cf0d1bffe816cf26b17d45c33a65af'
    assert result['symbols']['a']['interface_hash'] == new
    assert result['symbols']['a']['closure_hash'] == new
for p,q,c in product(range(2),range(2),range(4)):
    result = json.loads(run([REF,'run-action',scratch,'a','--mut',f'{p},{q}','--in',str(c)]).stdout)
    assert result['mut'] == [q ^ int(c==3),p] and result['in'] == [c]
    back = json.loads(run([REF,'run-action',scratch,'a','--mut',
                          ','.join(map(str,result['mut'])),'--in',str(c),'--adjoint']).stdout)
    assert back['mut'] == [p,q] and back['in'] == [c]
print('PASS: mcx replay proof; original reference rejection; ccx check/hash and 16+16 executions')
print('PASS: derive3 verification complete; proposals remain unaccepted/unimplemented')

## Remaining decisions and ownership

Council/editor owners must ratify and publish these changes; the existing
five issues remain their tracking records. Arith still owes exact incoming
conversion/FMA mechanics and flags after the parity decision. Runtimes
and driver/plugin still owe the helper protocol and bodies. Checker and
executor owners must add the proposed context/operand vectors, and the
K13 editor must publish a complete corrected fixture. None of that
downstream implementation is claimed complete by this notes-only job.

exec
/bin/bash -lc 'cat spec/README.md' in /home/tobias/Projects/bennett-workspace/bennett-isa-spec
 succeeded in 0ms:
# spec/

`Bennett-ISA-Spec.md` is the normative text: draft 0.8, **accepted by the
three-model council on 2026-10-28 (round 8, all members accept, no blocking
issues, mean 4.17/5)**. Council records and the runner live in the `bennett`
meta repo (`docs/council/isa-2026-10-28/`, `tools/council.py`).

Rules for changing it:

- Every semantic change bumps the full version string (section 1); drafts
  are not envelope-compatible, so the four example hashes in section 16.3
  change with it. Run `python3 reference-interpreter/isa_check.py --repatch`
  then `python3 reference-interpreter/isa_check.py`.
- Surgical edits only; a rewrite has introduced a mechanical error every
  time it was tried.
- Add every new example or stated result to `isa_check.py` before anyone
  grades it.
- Section 17 lists decisions deferred to implementation with their
  defaults; an implementer who must choose something not covered there
  files an issue and the default goes into section 17 first.
- Revisions go back through the council.

1003:| `qinplace` | `qinplace @g @ginv tk x (ins..) (mem..) (args..)`; written `qinplace @g @ginv tk x` when all three lists are empty (and only then) | **Well-formed** iff `@g` and `@ginv` are oracles whose `in` slots are x's width n followed by the widths of `ins` in order, whose `tgt` width is n, whose fault widths are equal (F), whose `mem` slots match the `mem` regions (role `in`, §3.3's matching rule) and whose `args` types are those of `args`; x, the `ins` wires and the effective control context are pairwise disjoint. **Sem**: `t = qalloc any n; f = qalloc any F; oracle_call @g tk (x, ins..) t f (mem..) (args..); qexch x t; oracle_call @ginv tk (x, ins..) t f (mem..) (args..); qfree t; qfree f`, so x ← g(x; ins, mem, args); these are its generated operations, in this order (§11). **Inverse-pair obligation**: under the tuple of `tk`, the call's `args` values and the tuples of the tokens among them, on every basis value of x, of the `ins` wires and of the `mem` cells, both oracles are fault-free and g(·; ins, mem, args) and ginv(·; ins, mem, args) are mutually inverse permutations of the n-bit encoding. It is carried by an `inverse_pairs` entry (§12.3) of the certificate record of the region whose body contains the `qinplace`; an entry `{g: G, ginv: H}` covers both `qinplace @G @H` and `qinplace @H @G` (its claim checks both compositions). An entry **covers** a `qinplace` when its `budget` is componentwise ≤ the tuple of `tk` (success at a tuple implies the same result at every larger one, §5.4), every token-typed argument's tuple equals the entry's `arg_tokens` tuple for that argument (equality: a nested oracle's outcome may change with its budget), and every other `args` value lies in its claim's `args` domain. Its claim is an `exhaustive` claim with `expected: "inverse_pair"`, which the executor verifies as a checker does (once per closure hash, before first use), or a `structural` `inverse_pair_library` claim, which covers when the named claim of the linked library's certificate is such a claim for the same closure hashes of g and ginv (no re-execution; a library claim without an `args` domain covers every value; its proof or evidence label is the library claim's); a `sampled` claim never covers. Before any effect of a CC/R region call, every `qinplace` the callee executes (transitively; its token and `args` are resolved from the call's classical arguments, which reach lifted calls only as classical arguments, §4.2) MUST have a covering entry, else CC trap / R fault `FAULT_UB_TRAP`. The private t and f are not values of the region for §6: the certified pair cleans them, the only cleanup that is not a §6.4 realisation. # 🚨 SESSION CLOSE PROTOCOL 🚨

**CRITICAL**: Before saying "done" or "complete", you MUST run this checklist:

```
[ ] 1. bd close <id1> <id2> ...   (close completed issues)
[ ] 2. run quality gates        (tests, linters, builds when relevant)
[ ] 3. git status               (check what changed)
[ ] 4. follow active profile    (conservative: report handoff; team-maintainer: commit/sync/push if enabled)
```

**Policy:** Conservative is the default. Commit, sync, or push only when the active user, orchestrator, or repository profile grants that authority.

## Core Rules
- **Default**: Use beads for ALL task tracking (`bd create`, `bd ready`, `bd close`)
- **Prohibited**: Do NOT use TodoWrite, TaskCreate, or markdown files for task tracking
- **Workflow**: Create beads issue BEFORE writing code, mark in_progress when starting
- **Memory**: Use `bd remember "insight"` for persistent knowledge across sessions. Do NOT use MEMORY.md files — they fragment across accounts. Search with `bd memories <keyword>`.
- Persistence you don't need beats lost context
- Default: do not commit, push, or run dolt remote sync without explicit authority. Team-maintainer behavior is opt-in and still subordinate to user/orchestrator instructions.
- Git workflow: conservative by default; commit/push only with explicit user/orchestrator or team-maintainer authority
- Session management: check `bd ready` for available work

## Essential Commands

### Finding Work
- `bd ready` - Show issues ready to work (no blockers)
- `bd list --status=open` - All open issues
- `bd list --status=in_progress` - Your active work
- `bd show <id>` - Detailed issue view with dependencies

### Creating & Updating
- `bd create --title="Summary of this issue" --description="Why this issue exists and what needs to be done" --type=task|bug|feature --priority=2` - New issue
  - Priority: 0-4 or P0-P4 (0=critical, 2=medium, 4=backlog). NOT "high"/"medium"/"low"
- `bd create ... --parent=<id>` - Hierarchical child (task under epic, subtask under task; inherits parent labels)
- `bd update <id> --claim` - Claim work
- `bd unclaim <id>` - Release stuck issue (agent crashed)
- `bd update <id> --assignee=username` - Assign to someone
- `bd update <id> --if-assignee=<expected> --assignee=<new>` - Atomic reassign: applies only if the assignee still matches (--if-status=<expected> guards status; --if-assignee='' requires unassigned). Mismatch exits non-zero with nothing written — never retry blindly
- `bd update <id> --title/--description/--notes/--design` - Update fields inline
- `bd close <id>` - Mark complete
- `bd close <id1> <id2> ...` - Close multiple issues at once (more efficient)
- `bd close <id> --reason="explanation"` - Close with reason
- **Tip**: When creating multiple issues/tasks/epics, use parallel subagents for efficiency
- **WARNING**: Do NOT use `bd edit` - it opens $EDITOR (vim/nano) which blocks agents

### Dependencies & Blocking
- `bd dep add <issue> <depends-on>` - Add dependency (issue depends on depends-on)
- `bd blocked` - Show all blocked issues
- `bd show <id>` - See what's blocking/blocked by this issue

### Sync & Collaboration
- `bd search <query>` - Search issues by keyword

### Project Health
- `bd stats` - Project statistics (open/closed/blocked counts)
- `bd doctor` - Check for issues (sync problems, missing hooks)
- `bd doctor --check=conventions` - Check for convention drift (lint, stale, orphans)

### Quality Tools
- `bd create --validate` - Check description has required sections
- `bd create --acceptance="criteria"` - Set acceptance criteria (checked by --validate)
- `bd create --design="decisions"` - Record design decisions
- `bd create --notes="context"` - Add supplementary notes
- `bd config set validation.on-create warn` - Auto-validate on every create
- `bd lint` - Check existing issues for missing sections

### Lifecycle & Hygiene
- `bd defer <id> --until="date"` - Defer work to a future date
- `bd supersede <id> --with=<new-id>` - Mark issue as superseded
- `bd close <id> --suggest-next` - Show newly unblocked issues after closing
- `bd stale` - Find issues with no recent activity
- `bd orphans` - Find issues with broken dependencies
- `bd preflight` - Pre-PR checks (lint, stale, orphans)
- `bd human <id>` - Flag for human decision (list/respond/dismiss)

### Structured Workflows
- `bd formula list` - See available workflow templates
- `bd mol pour <name>` - Start structured workflow from formula

 succeeded in 4ms:
### 3.4 Execution in CC and R

Sequential small-step execution with an instruction pointer, block
parameters instead of phi nodes, dynamic control flow and allocation;
every executed instruction pushes a run-history frame (§7.4).

**Names and definitions.** Every name used in a CC or R function is a
parameter, a block parameter or the result of at least one defining
instruction of that function, and all its definitions give it one type
(suite F). The text order of blocks is irrelevant: a definition need not
precede its uses textually. Executing a use of a name that has no
current instance (none was created on the executed path, or an `uncall`
removed it, §7.4) is a CC trap / R fault `FAULT_UB_TRAP`. Every CC or R
block ends in `br`, `ret`, `trap` or `fault` (suite F); `trap` and `fault`
end the run (§4.9).

### 3.5 Predication

Every Q instruction with unitary semantics executes controlled on the
active predicate. `qpred w` pushes, `qunpred w` pops the same wire; the
stack is empty at region entry and exit. Allocation, borrowing, release,
region creation and image gates are **unconditional**; only gates
(including those inside copy/XOR/exchange/memory instructions) are
controlled.

### 3.6 The predicated step machine

**Index space.** `I[0..n)` = the **rows** (one index per instruction
line) of the oracle's blocks, blocks in text order and rows in text
order within each block; then the rows of every lproc in its **lproc
closure** (transitively reachable through `lift.call`, linked ones
included), placed as follows: partition the closure into its SCCs
(§9.2); a one-member SCC's **order key** is its member's closure hash, a
multi-member SCC's order key is its closure `scc_hash`; SCCs are placed
in increasing bytewise order of their keys (64-hex), the members of a
multi-member SCC consecutively in SCC index order; each lproc's binding
instances (§3.7) consecutively in binding-key order, each instance's rows
in block text order as for the oracle. The oracle and each lproc
instance therefore occupy a contiguous range of indices, and a **label
`^b` denotes the index of the first row of block b**. `HALT = n`; 2^P > n. Branch and call
targets are labels; `w = lift.label ^b` is the only way a location
obtains an index value; executors MAY re-index dispatch extensionally
**provided every label value observable through `lift.label` keeps its
canonical numeric value and a `qjumpr` to any in-range index reaches the
row that canonical index names**. **Terminators**: `qjump qbranch qjumpr lift.
ret lift.retval fault`; every block ends in one; no label names HALT;
**only `lift.retval` reaches HALT with success**.

**Row classes.** Each §4.2 row is an **assignment** (A: written
locations/cells receive values computed out of place into frame slots
`disp_j`, then exchanged in, so `disp_j` retains the displaced old
value), a **direct** row (D: a controlled permutation in place), or
**mixed** (A+D) with an explicit ordered list of **elementary
sub-operations** printed in §3.7. **Slot kinds.** An **assignment slot**
receives a value computed out of place and is then exchanged with its
destination (it retains the displaced old value). A **capture slot**
receives a value computed out of place and is never exchanged (it
retains that value as history: the arguments of `lift.call`). The
**delivery slot** of `lift.ret` is a capture slot that is subsequently
exchanged, under a condition, with the destination location. Rows with
a **flag** compute it into `frame.flag` from the old state before any
effect; rows with a data-dependent successor compute it into `frame.
succ` before any effect; conditional-fault rows perform `faulted ^=
flag`, `code ^= flag·C′` after their other effects, where C′ is the
row's **effective cause** (§5.4: C′ = C, except C′ = `FAULT_UB_TRAP`
when a `lift.faultif` literal C is 0).

**Frame layout** (fixed per oracle): `(old_pc : wire[P], disp_1..disp_
{k_max}, flag : wire, succ : wire[P])`, `k_max` and widths `W_j` from the
widest write sets used (§4.2 lists each row's slot use).

**Forward machine step** t (1 ≤ t ≤ B), reads referring to the state at
the start of the step:

1. **Dispatch.** `at_k = (pc == k)` for k = 0..n into clean scratch.
2. **Execute.** For each k < n in index order: push `at_k`; run `I[k]`'s
   row: (a) compute `flag` into `frame_t.flag` if the row has one; (b)
   compute `succ` into `frame_t.succ` if data-dependent; (c) for A rows,
   compute **every** written value into its slot, then exchange **every**
   slot with its destination (all computes precede all exchanges); for D
   rows apply the row's permutation; for A+D rows follow the row's listed
   order; (d) apply the fault writes; pop `at_k`.
3. **Successor.** For each k < n, under `at_k`: XOR the row's successor
   into `pc'` (a label index under `flag`/`¬flag` as stated, or
   `frame_t.succ`). Under `at_HALT`: XOR `HALT` into `pc'`.
4. **Commit pc.** `qexch pc pc'`; `qexch pc' frame_t.old_pc` (now `pc` is
   the next index, `pc'` is clean, `old_pc` holds the old index).
5. **Clean dispatch.** Uncompute every `at_k` from `frame_t.old_pc`.
6. Decrement the classical step counter.

On an **idle** step (pc = HALT at its start) no row executes, step 3
XORs `HALT` into `pc'`, and step 4 leaves pc = HALT and `frame_t.old_pc` =
HALT; the inverse step clears it. (A variant that leaves idle frames at
zero is deferred, §17 item 17.)

**Inverse machine step** (prefix reversal runs it for t = B..1), in
this order:

1. Recompute every `at_k` from `frame_t.old_pc`.
2. `qexch pc' frame_t.old_pc`; `qexch pc pc'` (restores the old `pc`;
   `pc'` again holds the successor value; `old_pc` is clean).
3. For each k < n under `at_k`: XOR the row's successor into `pc'` again,
   using `frame_t.flag` / `frame_t.succ`; under `at_HALT`: XOR `HALT` into
   `pc'` (`pc'` is now clean).
4. For each k < n in **reverse index order** under `at_k`: undo the fault
   writes (`faulted ^= flag`, `code ^= flag·C′`); then undo the row's
   effects. **D row**: its sub-operations in reverse order, each by its
   own inverse (`lift.perm_call` by the callee's adjoint; XORs,
   controlled flips and exchanges by themselves). **A row**: exchange
   every assignment slot back with its destination **in the reverse of
   the forward exchange order** (the forward order is the row's listed
   slot order; every destination address, forward and inverse, is
   evaluated from the state current at that exchange, so an indexed
   destination such as `alloctab[atop]` is addressed with the counter
   value that the earlier inverse exchanges have already restored), then
   **uncompute every slot from the restored old state** (the same
   out-of-place computation, applied again as XOR, clears it).
   **A+D row**: **the exact reverse of its complete §3.7 sub-operation
   list**, each elementary sub-operation inverted at its reversed
   position: a direct sub-operation by its own inverse; an assignment
   sub-operation by exchanging its slot back and then uncomputing the
   slot; a capture sub-operation by uncomputing the slot (its source has
   been restored by the sub-operations already undone); the delivery
   sub-operation by the same conditional exchanges. **Every address and
   condition is evaluated from the state current at that position of the
   inverse** (so the `lift.ret` stack addresses and delivery condition
   use the callee `fp` restored one position earlier, and the `lift.call`
   record base uses the restored `sp`); §3.7 prints both inverse lists.
   Finally uncompute `frame_t.succ` and `frame_t.flag` from the restored
   old state.
5. Uncompute every `at_k` from `pc` (which now equals the old index).

After the inverse step every frame field is clean. *(non-normative)* The
forward step is a permutation Π_t; the inverse step is Π_t⁻¹ written
out; T2 relies on nothing else. B = 0 is legal: the oracle commits
`FAULT_BUDGET_STEPS` on every input.

### 3.7 Register files, procedures, counters, stack

## 1. Scope and conformance

### 1.1 What the ISA is

A program model for classical, reversible and coherent computation with
one semantics, executed by the VM (`bennett-vm`) and the circuit
specialiser (`bennett-circuits`), produced by the plugin
(`bennett-plugin`) through the dialect's reference semantics, scheduled
through §11, described for hardware by target descriptions (§8). Nothing
here refers to LLVM.

### 1.2 Conformance classes

| Class | Who | Obligation |
|---|---|---|
| Executor | VM, specialiser, hardware stacks | §3–§7, §14 exactly, and the checking of inverse-pair claims (§4.5, §12.1; a certificate applies when its `closure_hash` binding equals the region's); suites A–E, H |
| Producer | plugin, library compilers | well-formed objects (§9, §16) and regions (§5, §6); suite F |
| Scheduler | `bennett-sched` and competitors | §11; only schedules valid under §6; suite H |
| Checker | `bennett-conformance` | validates certificates (§12) without trusting producers |
| Target author | vendors | target descriptions (§8); suite E |

### 1.3 Deferred *(asserted non-semantic)*

Binary encodings (§16 text is the interchange form); mechanisation of
§14; device models beyond §7.3; the gate-name registry process; the
memory layout of the opaque C objects of §10–§11 (every constructor,
accessor and destructor is listed). **Minimality claims** (north star
§5) are not supported by cert 0.9 and MUST NOT appear in a certificate.
Decisions deferred to implementation, each with its default, are listed
in §17.

## 2. Terminology

- **Tier.** CC, R, Q. **Wire**, **bundle** `wire[N]` (little-endian).
- **Location** `reg[N]`: a named mutable cell of an oracle or lproc
  register file. **Pseudo-location**: an oracle interface input or
  classical argument, or an lproc classical parameter (§3.7), readable
  only in its declaring function, never written.
- **Region**: a Q function with a declared interface: **lifted oracle**
  (§5.2) or **quantum action** (§5.3); **permutation action** (§5.3).
- **lproc**: a lifted procedure with its own params and locations; it
  resolves names only to its own params and locations (§3.7).
- **Budget**: classical (B, heap_limit, stack_limit, depth_limit).
- **Frame**: per machine step: `old_pc`, slots (assignment, capture and
  delivery slots, §3.6), `flag`, `succ`.
- **Basis configuration**: classical values on every component of D.
- **Gate classes.** Permutation: `x cx ccx mcx swap`. Diagonal: `s sdg t
  tdg cz rz`. Non-diagonal: `h rx ry`.
- **Verifying executor**: an executor that can decide exactly whether a
  set of wires is clean on every basis configuration (a state-vector or
  branch-enumeration simulator); every other executor (hardware) is
  **non-verifying**. Rules that depend on the difference say which
  applies (§3.3 `qreg.free`, §10, §17 item 14).
- **Effective control context** at a point: the set of wires on the
  active predicate stack plus the inherited control context (the control
  wires of every enclosing `ctl` and predicate stack of every enclosing
  invocation, transitively); duplicates count once. Its size is the
  **effective control count**.

## 3. Machine model

### 3.1 Tiers and the control invariant

```
  CC ──calls──▶ R functions (call/uncall)       CC ──calls──▶ Q regions
  R  ──calls──▶ R functions, Q regions          Q  ──calls──▶ Q regions (§5.5); oracle bodies call lprocs
```

CC runs on the device's classical-control processor; the host reaches
the device only through §10. **No instruction in any tier makes a
control or scheduling decision that depends on the value of a wire**
except through predication (§3.5). Gate angles are finite `f64` literals
or classical arguments fixed at region entry.

**Call-tier policy** (suite F). The diagram, completed with same-tier
calls; a call, uncall or region call outside this table is ill-formed:

| Caller | `call` (§4.1) | `uncall` of a completed segment (§7.4) | region calls (§4.7) | lifted calls (§4.2) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CC function | CC and R functions | of a CC or R callee | oracles, actions | – |
| R function | R functions | of an R callee | oracles, actions | – |
| oracle body, lproc | – | – | – | `lift.call` of lprocs; `lift.oracle_call`/`lift.oracle_uncall` of oracles; `lift.perm_call` of permutation actions |
| action | – | – | oracles, actions (§5.3, §5.5) | – |

An R function never calls a CC function: CC code may measure and make
host requests (§4.1), which an R history cannot reverse.

### 3.2 State

| Component | Tier | Contents |
|---|---|---|
| classical environment | CC, R | dynamic SSA instances (§7.4), block parameters, activation stack, **quantum-register handles** (`qreg[n]`, `qreg[*]`, §3.3), tokens (§5.4) |
| register and token ledgers | CC, R | every register's id, class, width and state (§3.3); every device-minted token's tuple and validity (§5.4) |
| classical memory | CC, R | byte-addressed, little-endian, sparse; allocation table (§7.1) |
| effect tape, entropy tape | R | §7.3 |
| run history | CC, R | one frame per executed instruction (§7.4) |
| wire store | Q (CC and R via `qreg`) | live wires with roles `input target fault borrowed mutable workspace location cell` |
| quantum memory | Q | regions of `wire[W]` cells: local (§4.6) or interface (§5.1) |
| register file | Q | locations of the active oracle and its lproc closure (§3.7) |
| predicate stack | Q | wires; the active predicate is their conjunction |
| oracle step state | Q | `pc, pc' : wire[P]`; `faulted : wire`; `code : wire[F]`; `result_val : wire[T]`; counters (§3.7); B frames |
| budget | Q | classical: B, heap_limit, stack_limit, depth_limit |

### 3.3 Types, ownership, operand schema

```
  iN (1 ≤ N ≤ 128)   ptr (width ptr_width)   f64   token   qreg[n]   qreg[*]   region[A,W]
  wire   wire[N]      linear values in structured code and interfaces
  reg[N]              a location (oracle bodies, lprocs); not linear
```

**Widths.** The bound 1 ≤ N ≤ 128 applies to the type `iN` only (a lifted
row's `.iN` suffix names a location width, §4.2). `wire[N]`,
`reg[N]` and `qreg[n]` have N, n ≥ 1 and `region[A,W]` has A ≥ 0, W ≥ 1,
with no upper bound (a `qreg[*]` register's width is a run-time value n
≥ 0, §3.3 below); a value of these types is a bit-vector of its
width in every semantics (§14.1), including interface bundles,
pseudo-locations, locations, cells, stack cells and frame slots.

**Linear SSA with implicit versioning** (structured code and CC region
calls): a `wire`/`wire[N]` value is produced once and consumed once;
mode `in` consumes and re-produces a successor of the same name;
`consume` consumes; `produce` creates; a name is not used after
`consume`. **Regions are threaded the same way.** **Indexed operand**
`w[i]` (literal i) borrows wire i for one instruction.

**Disjointness.** The wire operands of one instruction are pairwise
distinct; **every wire of the effective control context is distinct from
every target and from every consumed or produced operand** of an
instruction executed under it (so `qpred p; x p` is ill-formed); a
predicate wire is not modified between its push and its matching pop; a
`qif`/`qloop … while` selector is not modified inside its arms or body.
A region's interface bundles and regions are pairwise disjoint.
Distinctness is judged on the wires denoted: a bundle and an indexed
operand of it, or two indexed operands denoting the same wire, overlap
and are not distinct. [deps: ISA-3.3-P3, ISA-4.4]

**Per-opcode operand schema** (§4.8): every opcode has a tier set, a
fixed arity, operand kinds and a result type; results are written first
(`w = op a b`), modes are never written, and **result types that are not
determined by the operands are written as a suffix** (`r = const.i32 5`,
`r = zext.i64 a`, `r = trunc.i8 a`, `r = ptrtoint.i64 p`, `p = inttoptr
a`). Width rules: `binop`: `iN` operands of equal width; `icmp`: `iN` or
`ptr` operands of equal width and the same type (CC/R; lifted forms:
§4.2); `select` arms equal width; `zext`/`sext` strictly widen, `trunc`
strictly narrows; `load.iN`
and `lift.load.iN` require N ∈ {8, 16, …, 128}; `measure` of `qreg[n]`
with n ≤ 128 yields `in`, and `measure.iN` (1 ≤ N ≤ 128) of a `qreg[*]`
yields `iN`; signed division truncates toward zero. The
literal c of `const.iN c` and `lift.const.iN c` satisfies −2^(N−1) ≤ c <
2^N and denotes c mod 2^N (both the signed and the unsigned spelling of
every value are accepted); any other literal is ill-formed (suite F).

**Lifted width rules** (suite F; a mismatch is ill-formed, never a
truncation or an extension). Every address operand (`a` of `lift.load`/
`lift.store`, `p` of `lift.boundscheck`/`lift.free`) and every address
result (`w` of `lift.addr`/`lift.alloc`) is `reg[ptr_width]`; the length
`n` of `lift.alloc`/`lift.boundscheck` is a location of any width or a
literal in [0, 2^ptr_width); `inrange` and every prospective sum are
computed at width max(widths of the operands involved) + 1 with no wrap.
The destination of `lift.label` and the operand of `qjumpr` have any
width N with 2^N > n (the index-space size). `lift.call` location
arguments have exactly the callee's parameter widths and its destination the callee's
result width; its token, classical and region lists match the callee's
corresponding groups in count, order and type (§3.7); `lift.ret v` has
the containing lproc's result width;
`lift.oracle_call`/`lift.oracle_uncall`/`lift.perm_call` operands have
exactly the callee signature widths, and `mem` operands name caller
regions whose `[A,W]` equal the callee's and whose role is `in` for an
`in` slot (an `in` region is never passed to a `mut` slot); `fault C` and
`lift.faultif p C` require the literal C < 2^F, where F is the fault
width of the containing oracle or, in an lproc, of every oracle whose
lproc closure contains it (checked at load and again at link; an lproc
in no oracle's closure is checked when linked into one). The equal-width and widening/narrowing constraints above apply to lifted rows on their location and pseudo-location bit-vectors as specified in §4.2; the CC/R iN and ptr operand-type restrictions do not retype lifted locations. The destinations of one `lift.parmov` are pairwise distinct.

**Quantum registers in CC and R.** A **register** is n device-resident
wires of one class with a nonzero 64-bit **id**, recorded in the
program's **register ledger** with its class, width and state:
**live**, **viewed** (one slice of it is live) or **consumed**.

 succeeded in 33ms:

### 16.1 Lexical

UTF-8; LF line endings; one instruction per line; tokens separated by
exactly one space; no trailing spaces; exactly one blank line before
each `section` line and none elsewhere. `NAME := [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_.]*`;
`INT := -?(0|[1-9][0-9]*)`; `FLOAT :=` shortest round-trip decimal with
`.` or exponent, finite; `STRING := "…"` with escapes `\"`, `\\`, `\n`;
`HEX := ([0-9a-f]{2})*`. Metadata lines are RFC 8785 canonical JSON
objects. Every integer token is an `INT` (`ptr_width`, type widths,
`bounds`, literal operands, data and region sizes), so `032`, `+3` and
`09` are ill-formed; every gate angle is a `FLOAT` whose spelling is the
ECMAScript Number-to-String rendering that RFC 8785 uses for numbers,
with `.0` appended when that rendering contains neither `.` nor `e`, and
`-0.0` for negative zero (`0.7`, `1.0`, `1e+21`, `-0.0`; never `0.70`,
`1.0e0` or `00.7`). Where §16.2 prints `SP` twice around an empty list
(`regs {  }`) that spelling is canonical. A data line whose `HEX` is
empty is `NAME SP LF`: its mandatory separator space is the sole
exception to the no-trailing-space rule. The text has no comment
syntax; annotations travel outside the object.

### 16.2 Grammar

```
  object    := header NL section*
  header    := "bennett-object" SP "isa=" VER SP "object=" VER SP "launch=" VER SP "sched=" VER SP "cert=" VER SP "coverage=" VER SP "target=" VER SP "ptr_width=" INT
  section   := NL "section" SP SECTNAME NL body
  SECTNAME  := "symbols" | "imports" | "functions" | "data" | "devices" | "layout" | "schedule" | "certificate" | "coverage"
  body      := jsonline | dataline* | function*
  jsonline  := JCS-canonical JSON object NL
  dataline  := NAME SP HEX NL
  function  := fhead NL regs? block+
  fhead     := ("fn" SP ("cc"|"r") | "oracle" | "action" | "perm_action" | "lproc") SP "@" NAME SP "(" plist ")" SP "(" plist ")" SP "(" plist ")" SP "(" plist ")" SP "(" plist ")"
               -- oracle: (in..) (tgt) (fault) (mem..) (args..) ; action: (mut..) (in..) (mem..) (args..) () ; lproc: (params..) (result) (tokens..) (classical..) (mem..) ; fn: (params..) (results..) () () ()
  plist     := (param ("," SP param)*)?
  param     := NAME ":" type (SP ("in"|"mut"|"token"|"classical"))?
  type      := "i" INT | "ptr" | "f64" | "token" | "wire" | "wire[" INT "]" | "reg[" INT "]" | "region[" INT "," INT "]" | "qreg[" (INT | "*") "]"
  regs      := "regs" SP "{" SP (NAME ":" type ("," SP NAME ":" type)*)? SP "}" (SP "bounds" SP "{" INT ("," SP INT)* "}")? NL
  block     := "^" NAME "(" plist "):" NL instr+
  instr     := (lhs SP "=" SP)? OPCODE (SP operand)* (SP "{" NL instr+ "}" (SP "{" NL instr+ "}")?)? NL
  lhs       := NAME | "(" NAME ("," SP NAME)* ")"
  operand   := NAME | NAME "[" INT "]" | "@" NAME | "^" NAME ("(" (NAME ("," SP NAME)*)? ")")? | INT | FLOAT | STRING | "(" (NAME ("," SP NAME)*)? ")"
  OPCODE    := a mnemonic of §4 with its dotted suffix (§4.8 fixes operand count, kinds and result placement)
  VER       := INT "." INT
```

The empty list operand `()` also spells the address-only empty actual of
§4.6, solely in the address position of its four coherent memory rows
when A = 0. It is not an ordinary wire value.

The oracle header and signature declare no invocation-token slot. Its
catalog interface follows the five printed groups exactly; the separate
`tk` operand binds the typed `oracle_invocation` channel of §11.1, not a
reserved header name or an extra `args` element.

Lproc groups have the types and roles of §3.7. A `token` suffix is
permitted only in the third group and a `classical` suffix only in the
fourth, each agreeing with that group's role; either suffix may be
omitted. The first and second groups carry no suffix, and the fifth
requires `in`. The `token` and `classical` suffixes are not permitted on
other kinds of function or on block parameters or `regs` entries. The additional
`lift.call` lists use the existing list operand grammar (§3.7).

### 4.3 Allocation

| Instruction | Form | Rules |
|---|---|---|
| `qalloc` | `w = qalloc cls n` | n clean wires of class `cls` or `any`; unconditional |
| `qborrow` | `w = qborrow cls n` | n wires in unknown state; usable only inside a borrow frame (§5.6) |
| `qfree` | `qfree w` | release a `qalloc`ed bundle clean on every basis configuration |
| `qreturn` | `qreturn w` | matched inverse of `qborrow` after its frame |

In addition to §3.3's operand rules, `qfree d` is well-formed iff
every wire's current version is its `qalloc` version (including a
version restored by a matched inverse) or the clean output of an
explicit §6.4 cleanup of the value occupying that wire.
[deps: ISA-3.3-P3, ISA-4.3-P1, ISA-5.1, ISA-6.4]

### 4.4 Gates

```
  x = [[0,1],[1,0]]   h = (1/√2)[[1,1],[1,-1]]   s = diag(1,i)   sdg = diag(1,-i)
  t = diag(1,e^{iπ/4})  tdg = diag(1,e^{-iπ/4})   cz = diag(1,1,1,-1)
  rx(θ) = exp(-iθX/2)   ry(θ) = exp(-iθY/2)   rz(θ) = exp(-iθZ/2) = diag(e^{-iθ/2}, e^{iθ/2})
  cx, ccx, mcx(k): X on the target iff all controls are |1⟩ (exact, no phase)    swap: exact exchange
```

Forms: `x a`, `cx c t`, `ccx c1 c2 t`, `mcx (c_1..c_k) t`, `swap a b`,
`h a`, `s a`, `sdg a`, `t a`, `tdg a`, `cz a b`, `rx θ a`, `ry θ a`, `rz θ
a`; all controlled on the active predicate. **Realisation**: permutation
status is a property of the realised unitary at the gate boundary;
conformant iff equal to the specified unitary exactly including global
phase (ε = 0) or within declared ε in operator norm. **Oracles and
permutation actions require ε = 0** for every gate; actions MAY use ε > 0
(§12). A relative-phase Toffoli is never a conformant `ccx`.

### 11.1 Reference-library contract (K11)

**ISA-REF-LIB** (K11; b-isa-spec-8r4, from bennett-vm-47t).
This is the decided 0.9 contract. The catalog bound as ref version 0.9
is the file `spec/ref-0.9.json` whose four-line envelope hashes to
`5de4e4995350313e50217880ba1b95de39cd043283b726337d4894efb005d197`.
It publishes the complete 219-recipe catalog, byte-identical to the
accepted catalog snapshot. Consumers lacking the bound library report
`E_SCHEDULE_INFEASIBLE` as §11 requires. The witness below is not a
full-library hash.

**Serialization and identity.** The schema and family witnesses are
documented in `spec/ref-0.9-draft.README.md`. The JSON file is RFC 8785 canonical
UTF-8, with no trailing LF, containing exactly `isa`, `name`, `version`
and `recipes`. The first three are strings `0.9`, `ref`, `0.9`.
`recipes` is an array in bytewise `kind` order, then increasing parameter
tuple order. Each instantiated recipe has `kind`, its integer width or
other named integer parameters, `operations`, `scratch`, `removable`
and `source` (the motivating ISA sentence). A parameterised recipe
uses `parameters` (an ordered array of formals defined below) and `body` instead of the
instance's `operations`; its body uses the closed constructors below.
Recipes cover every §4 instruction/row, the §3.6 step, §3.7 activation
operations and §5.2 construction, with no implementation-private leaf.
Classical instructions/effects remain classical leaf events (§8.2),
not invented quantum gates. The README documents schema, parameters,
coverage and witnesses; it is not hashed and cannot alter operations.

The library hash is SHA-256 of this UTF-8 envelope (every line ends LF,
including the last); the fourth line is the **entire** canonical library
JSON, including all recipes, sources and versions, without an embedded
hash field:

```
isa=0.9
expansion=ref
version=0.9
<JCS library document>
```

`bindings.expansion` names `ref`, version `0.9`, and this 64-lowercase-hex
hash. This adopts §9.2's version-prefixed, LF-terminated **envelope
convention**, with explicit expansion-domain and library-version lines;
it is a new library rule, not §9.2's symbol signature/SCC envelope.
Motivation (0.8 §11): "a versioned document, identified in the schedule's
`bindings.expansion` as { name, version, hash }"; 0.8 §9.2: "Semantic
envelope ... every part ends with LF". A changed recipe, source, scratch
name or ordering changes the library hash; no schedule/certificate can
silently inherit that change (§12.1's realised-subject binding).

**Expansion and ordinals.** Recipe bodies are ordered arrays
of constructors: a gate (`gate`, ordered `wires`, optional `params`), an
allocation (`alloc`, `width`, `class`, `state`), release (`release`),
classical event (`classical`, `point`), counted loop (`for`, `from`,
`to`, `body`), named recipe expansion (`invoke`, ordered `args`), or
inverse (`inverse`, `body`). Constructor objects have exactly those
keys. Fully instantiated `operations` contain only emitted leaves, each
also carrying its computed `ordinal`; unexpanded `body` constructors
carry no ordinal. Scalars are integer literals, parameter names, or prefix arrays
`[op, operands…]` for `+ - * div mod bit`; div is floor division with a
positive divisor. `bit(x,i)` requires nonnegative integers x and i and
returns floor(x/2^i) mod 2, with no fixed machine-word truncation (K23).
For x=13, bit(x,0..4) is 1,0,1,1,0; literal construction emits X only
for the one bits, in increasing bit order. Wires are declaration names with integer-expression
subscripts. Loops enumerate [from,to) in increasing order; expansion is
depth first, array order, and recipe dependencies are acyclic. An
inverse reverses its fully expanded body and adjoints each leaf by
§5.3, interchanging allocation/release. The complete serialized catalog
must encode the algorithms below in these constructors before release;
this document does not claim that prose alone is an interchangeable file.

**Typed binding (K19).** A formal is `{ "name": name, "type": type }`,
with exactly these keys, except `wire`, which additionally requires
`width`, and `templates`, which additionally requires `length` (integer
expressions using earlier integer formals); `arguments` requires `symbol`,
and `value` requires `isa_type`, as specified in K24; K25–K27 add
`records`, `region`, `class`, and `allocation` with the keys specified there. Types are `integer`,
`wire`, `angle`, `body`, `symbol`, `environment`, `event`, `image`,
`templates`, `arguments`, `value`, `records`, `region`, `class`, and
`allocation`. A bare name abbreviates an
`integer` formal, retaining the printed witness bytes. Names are unique
within a parameter list. Width expressions use only earlier integer
formals. A wire formal of width n is the ordered list w[n], with
little-endian indexing, including the empty list when n = 0.

`invoke` binds `args` positionally, evaluating actuals in the caller's
lexical environment before binding any callee formal. Integer actuals use
the scalar grammar above. A wire actual is a wire name, a subscripted
wire, an array of wire actuals concatenated left to right, or exactly
`{ "range": name, "from": scalar, "to": scalar }`, selecting [from,to)
from a named bundle; bounds must satisfy 0 ≤ from ≤ to ≤ its width.
An actual's resulting length must equal its formal's evaluated width.
In a gate's `wires` array a whole formal emits all its wires in order;
this encodes `mcx(k)` as k controls followed by one target, including k=0.
`{ "each": name, "in": actual, "body": [...] }` additionally visits
wire-list elements in order, binding name as a one-wire formal for each
visit. Its binding shadows lexically and is restored after the loop.

A body actual is an ordered constructor array, captured with its caller's
lexical environment, or the name of an existing body formal. Exactly
`{ "splice": name }` expands it at that point. Substitution is
capture-free: caller references never resolve to callee locals, loop and
`each` bindings have lexical scope, and every fresh allocation identity
is derived from its expansion occurrence path (including loop iteration,
body-splice occurrence and invocation path). Allocations must not shadow a
live declaration in the same scope; a release names its matching live
allocation. An inverse traverses constructors in reverse, toggles the
adjoint on nested bodies and uses the same restored bindings at each
position; allocation declarations supply release/adjoint metadata even
when allocation and release occur in different nested constructor lists.

A symbol actual is a 64-lowercase-hex §9.2 closure hash, or a bound symbol
formal. The linked closure registry supplies that symbol's own typed
interface and constructor body; the hash must match the linked closure,
not an arbitrary supplied body. Exactly `{ "call": name, "args": [...] }`
expands that symbol under a new invocation-path element identified by the
call constructor's source point, containing fixed-binding instance and
forward semantic-call occurrence number as serialized in §11. [deps: ISA-3.7-P5, ISA-3.7-P6, ISA-11, ISA-11.1-P29]
It retains its own ordinal space starting at zero. [deps: ISA-11-P2, ISA-11.1-P29.S2]
`invoke` is recipe composition within a row; `call` is a semantic symbol
invocation and never flattens callee ordinals into the caller's space. [deps: ISA-11-P2, ISA-11.1-P29.S2]
Repeated calls have distinct paths through their `call_index`, even when
callee hash and arguments agree. [deps: ISA-11, ISA-11.1-P29.S2]

The inherited effective-control set is always passed to callees and body
splices. An `environment` actual uses the wire-actual grammar and adds
its wires to that set (set union, repeated controls counted once).
Exactly `{ "under": actual, "body": [...] }` adds controls for its lexical
body and restores the outer set afterwards. Controls apply to semantic
gates only, never allocation/release or classical events (§3.5/§4.3).
A control may coincide with a gate's explicit control, but never with a
target (including a diagonal-gate target), as §3.3 requires. Gate operands
must otherwise be distinct.

An `event` actual is exactly `{ "instruction": text, "point": text }`,
or an event formal name. `instruction` is one complete canonical §9.1
classical instruction with its operand bindings; `point` identifies its
§8.2 classical execution point. `{ "classical": opcode, "point":
event_name }` emits that bound event (its opcode must match, allowing a
concrete `.iN` width suffix) without executing or substituting its
instruction text; classical semantics and retained history are §4.1/§7.4.
Literal classical leaves retain their existing `classical`/`point` form.
A classical event is not a unitary; an `inverse` containing one requires
retained §7.4 history and is not a quantum-library matrix witness.

Consumers MUST reject the library document on arity/type/width mismatch,
unbound name or closure hash, invalid wire range, duplicate formal,
nonfinite angle, unknown constructor/key/gate, invalid gate arity,
coincident target/control, unmatched allocation/release, or cyclic
`invoke`/symbol expansion. Static integer bounds must yield finite
expansion; validation is repeated for every actual parameter tuple before
its expansion can be used. There are no implicit bindings or defaults;
K82 below is the only by-name binding, and it is explicit.
Acyclicity concerns declared
recipe/symbol dependencies, not repetition of a higher-order recipe name
inside finite captured actual syntax: qloop(3, qloop(2, B)) expands to six
copies of B. Consumers expand finite actual bodies to a constructor fixpoint.

**Named-interface recipes (K82).** A recipe whose `parameters` are all
bare names, including an empty list, is a named-interface recipe. Its
named interface is every name its body uses as a wire before any lexical
binding of that name, in body order, plus the named interface of each
named-interface recipe it invokes, less the names bound at that invoke.
Every other recipe, and every K22 template even with bare formals, is
closed: a name it leaves unbound rejects.
An `invoke` of a named-interface recipe evaluates each interface name as
a wire actual in the invoke's lexical environment, under the same name,
when it evaluates `args`. K26 and K27 apply as for any wire actual, and an
invoke inside a captured body resolves in that capture. A name with no
wire binding there rejects. Nothing else is imported: an integer, body or
other non-wire name stays unbound in the callee, and any other caller name
is invisible to it. The callee's allocations belong to its own scope, so
they do not shadow an unrelated caller name.
An interface name has no declared width. Each subscript must lie within
the bound list, and a whole-name use takes the entire list under the
gate's arity check; the bound list may be longer than the body reads.
Equal-width checking belongs to the typed formals of the invoking recipe.
Instantiating such a recipe other than by `invoke` requires an explicit
map from exactly its interface names to wire lists. A body that releases
an interface name still requires its matching live allocation.
In `ref` version 0.9, 54 recipes have this form. Exactly 20 `invoke` sites
reach them, each from a typed recipe whose wire formals carry the identical
names, and none from another named-interface recipe. This rule changes no
catalog byte, leaf, ordinal or hash.
Witness: `bind.value.add(2,x,y,z)` binds typed formals a, b, t to x, y, z;
its `invoke` of `value.add(2)` then reads those same three lists, emitting
the leaves of `value.add` over x, y, z. A typed recipe reading a formal it
does not declare rejects, as does an invoke with no `q` in scope of a recipe
reading `q`.

**Image binding (K21).** An `image` actual is a bound image formal or
an even-length string of lowercase hexadecimal byte pairs in data-image
